### Window-management reference


Rows now keep per-item widths stable across insertion/removal/reordering and reveal overflow through scrolling on their own output. The shared `item.cycle-width` command, default Mod+R, uses `DesktopAction::CycleItemWidth` and `Workspace::cycle_item_width`; Operation's `/item/width` invokes that same action. The cycle is **1 → 1/2 → 1/3 → 2/3 → 1**, with no key auto-repeat. A half-width item advances to one third, two thirds, full, then half. Arbitrary/manual sizes and removed presets start at the first entry. Ratios are compared by exact intent, so client minimums producing equal pixel sizes cannot trap the cycle. Maximize restores normal state and advances from full; fullscreen reports that it must be left first. Height, row membership, neighbors and client identity remain intact.

**Park → Layout · Window layout** opens the implemented native modal with percentage/fraction fields, Add/Remove, Up/Down, a separate default opening width, gaps, application-opening policy, Save and Cancel. Retain this shared the settings and window-management references editor rather than adding a competing size editor. It initially displays 100%, 50%, 1/3 and 2/3, preserving exact thirds instead of round-tripping approximate percentages. Validation rejects empty/invalid/equivalent presets and bounds the list to 1–32. Save uses the command/configuration reference's atomic Lingua transaction and preserves current bindings; validation/write/revision failures retain the same draft entities, focus and scroll. Changing presets does not resize existing items. Newly managed items use the configured normal-width default, initially half, with the separate opening-state policy applied as the settings and window-management references specify. Their neighbors retain their widths. The settings summary shows the focused rectangle's current logical width separately from its requested preset, including constrained sizes. The layout and shortcut modals share `KeyboardModalOwner`, compositor handoff markers and held-key quarantine on close; native configured bindings, underlying outside-modal binding scopes and compositor shortcuts remain suspended while either owns input.

The interaction contract is one width-cycle command, one press per step, shared by normal application windows and eligible native items. Starting with a full-width item, the accepted default sequence is:

| Separate Mod+R presses | Requested horizontal width |
| --- | --- |
| Before pressing | 1 (100%) |
| 1 | 1/2 (50%) |
| 2 | 1/3 (33⅓%) |
| 3 | 2/3 (66⅔%) |
| 4 | 1 (100%); the cycle repeats |

Holding the chord does not race through the list. Ratios refer to the current output's usable width after bar reservations and gaps, rather than the width of an arbitrary neighboring item. Full width changes horizontal size only; it does not request fullscreen, hide bars or change row height. In free/floating placement, use the existing width solver and retained scale intent instead of tying client resize requests to camera motion. Represent the requested 0.33/0.66 examples as exact 1/3 and 2/3 by default. Users can replace and reorder the fractions in the existing modal; the chord is editable in the shared shortcut editor. The default opening width is a separate preference, initially half; editing the cycle must not resize existing windows. No additional preset buttons or dedicated chord per size are required.

Keep the everyday path short: select an item and press Mod+R repeatedly; change the ordered sizes through **Park → Layout → Window layout**, or change the chord through the existing command binding editor. The command steps through the saved list without opening a chooser on each press. The settings summary must make both destinations discoverable and show the effective saved chord. The modal's existing Add/Remove and Up/Down controls are sufficient; do not introduce another size menu or require a configuration-file edit. Keyboard bindings and the ordered preset list persist through the same interface configuration transaction, while each item's current requested width stays with its existing workspace layout state.

Use these as the two everyday acceptance paths: an existing full-width window needs four separate presses to show half, third, two-thirds and full again; a newly opened normal half-width window needs three presses to reach full. The latter starts at its retained half-width intent rather than restarting the cycle at full. Opening maximized remains a separate state with the restoration behavior below. Both paths use the same command and saved ordered list, without choosing a column mode or opening settings for each resize.

Keep custom input distinct from the default examples: entering `0.33` or `33%` deliberately stores 33/100, while entering `1/3` stores one third. Do not silently convert an owner's decimal choice to the nearest default fraction. Display the exact retained value when reopening the editor, and reject equivalent duplicates such as `0.5` and `1/2` with a useful validation message while preserving the draft. Editing presets configures the cycle; invoking the width command is what resizes the focused item.

Count input presses rather than rendered frames: a complete modifier/chord press and release arriving in one update must still advance once, while two distinct presses in that update must advance twice. OS key-repeat events must not add steps. Exercise this through the shared input bridge and a saved replacement chord, preserving the modifier state associated with each event rather than inferring every event from the frame's final held keys. This is part of the existing command/input implementation, not a separate width-key handler.

When a tiled item grows, retain the other items' widths and reveal the focused item through that row's existing viewport. Do not shrink its neighbors or zoom the entire workspace to fit the row. With no eligible focused item, report the command as unavailable through the same registry/help path and leave the cycle and view unchanged. Rebinding Mod+R must preserve these semantics: the cycle belongs to the typed command, not a hardcoded key handler.

Treat an empty workspace and an explicitly unfocused workspace containing eligible items as separate acceptance cases. In the second case the width action must not select the nearest item, restore a previous focus, enter focus-follow or resize anything implicitly. Check availability again when the typed action executes, so changing focus after opening Operation cannot apply a stale resize target. Preserve the requested width intent, camera, item rectangles and explicit Unfocus state on rejection; return the same useful reason to the button, shortcut and Operation entry points.

The cycle follows the item's requested preset, not its closest measured pixel width. The following cases define the command's state transitions and its integrated acceptance:

| Starting state | Result of one width-cycle press |
| --- | --- |
| New item with the default half-width intent | Request one third, the next entry in the configured order |
| Manual horizontal resize, with no retained preset | Request the first configured entry, initially full width |
| Manual vertical resize or position-only movement | Advance from the retained horizontal preset; neither action resets it |
| Current preset removed from the saved list | Request the first entry in the new list on the next press; saving the list itself leaves geometry unchanged |
| Current preset retained but reordered | Find that exact fraction in the new order and advance from it; do not retain an obsolete numeric index |
| Several presets constrained to the same effective client width | Advance the requested fraction once even when the effective size does not change |
| Last entry in the configured list | Wrap to the first entry; a one-entry list remains on that entry |
| Currently maximized item | Leave maximize and advance from the full-width preset, preserving the normal height and row |
| Fullscreen or no eligible focused item | Explain the unavailable action and leave placement, camera and focus unchanged |

Keep the focused-width summary explicit about requested proportion and effective logical size, and test this distinction with a constrained Wayland client rather than changing the preset order to hide it. Existing pure/model checks remain evidence for their covered cases; extend only missing integrated client/startup coverage instead of rebuilding the width feature.

Use a concrete integration fixture to make this distinction observable: with zero gaps, a usable width of 900 logical units and a client minimum width of 450, the requested full/half/third/two-thirds/full sequence resolves to 900/450/450/600/900. The repeated 450 is valid; the next press must still advance to two-thirds. Moving that item to another output retains its requested fraction and resolves against the destination's usable width, preserving its live surface and neighboring items. Restart restores the configured cycle and saved width intent through their existing configuration/workspace owners.

`layout/desktop/widths.rs` resolves rational widths against usable bounds with inter-item gap allowance, so two halves or three thirds fit without cumulative gap error. `Item::free_width` stores optional ratio intent plus the canvas scale at invocation for free/floating rectangles. Re-resolving output/bar bounds preserves that scale and the item's center/height; ordinary camera zoom does not resize clients every frame. Manual width changes clear the preset intent, while movement and representational float rounding preserve it. Floating homes cache the authored and floating width intents separately, alongside their respective geometry. Native layout updates the free rectangle's fixed-width rule so the subsequent authored-layout pass cannot undo the resize. During geometry reconciliation, compare each observed size axis with the last presented geometry: a position-only update preserves a pending model resize and its center, while an actual observed size change takes precedence as a manual resize. This prevents physics or dragging from copying a previous frame's width over a new command. Arbitrary imported scene models remain outside this window-layout path.

Use the implemented shared entry points when extending this work:

```rust
DesktopAction::CycleItemWidth.apply(world, root);
model.cycle_item_width(&layout.width_presets, usable, canvas.zoom)?;
```

The first call is the native command entry; the second is its solver-level operation, not an additional action to dispatch after the first. The existing fixed-column cycle is internal support only. All 62 layout tests and 34 shortcut/configuration/editor tests pass (`ergonomics-widths-layout-tests-v4.log`, `ergonomics-widths-settings-tests-v5.log`), covering constraints, gaps, wraparound, free/floating state, restart persistence, native controls, stale saves, plain Escape from an edited field and suppression through held-key release. The release width-modal GPU test passes actual text replacement, duplicate validation, reordering, saving at 360 pixels, reopening the underlying Park panel and Escape cancellation (`ergonomics-final-gpu-release.log`). Wide and 360-pixel captures were inspected. The edit panel now clamps its width/anchor to the usable viewport even though Edit is the leftmost toolbar control; all 13 edit-panel tests pass (`ergonomics-widths-edit-tests-v3.log`). End-to-end real-client/provider/hardware acceptance remains in stage 4.

The shared opening/closing foundation is implemented. Application insertion preserves ordinary focus and explicit Unfocus without a reveal operation; actual dialog unmap restores an eligible parent; authorized activation uses the existing workspace/output transaction; interactive launch commands supply a fresh standard token. Existing transient placement, the settings reference opening/maximize preferences, restored owner properties and ordinary neighboring-item fallback stay on their original paths. V5 covers those endpoints, and v22 below verifies a natural Qt-owned dialog through its actual controls and input. Stage 4 retains delayed close during native settings/capture, optimized and installed acceptance, and stage 2 retains the additional opening-rule controls.

The remaining toolkit/native capture handoff is complete at v31/v32. Both real Qt workflows pass in **128.52s**, covering actual Mod+P and `/help`, active-resize cancellation, native shortcut capture, held Ctrl+F2 quarantine, normal text input after return, an owned dialog closing asynchronously while capture retains the keyboard and subsequent parent typing. Both projections also verify the dialog's centered placement. The three new placement model tests pass. V31's ordinary Park suite passes **213 tests** with **74 explicitly ignored**, and shared topology passes **132 tests** with one ignored. All five source gates pass with warnings denied: all Park targets/features **18.03s**, nested/server-tests **7.47s**, server-only tests **2.13s**, GUI `instinct,park` **11.09s** and Sandbox `instinct` **8.96s**. The broad desktop run exposed an old screen-clamping expectation for free-canvas dialogs; v32 replaces it with independent authored-displacement expectations and adds a separate oversized tiled-dialog bound check. All **132 desktop tests pass in 1.28s**, after **1m13s** compilation. Preserve `ergonomics-toolkit-functional-v31.log`, the v31 source/regression logs, `ergonomics-toolkit-regression-v32-desktop.log`, v31/v32 scoped source archives and frozen executable hashes, and `toolkit-v31/review.png`. This closes the focused the window-management reference checkbox; optimized, mixed-DPI, installed and physical acceptance remain in stage 4/delivery.

The accepted scenario is implemented in `toolkit/handoff.rs`, extending the same external Qt fixture and production `Desktop::accept`/presentation pump. Use actual Mod+P from Qt, type `/help` and Enter, then reach the existing Shortcuts summary and capture controls by ordinary pointer/wheel input. An active resize first cancels as Operation takes ownership; release its quarantined button before clicking settings, rather than bypassing that legitimate grab with a direct action. Cancel the capture draft with Ctrl+F2 still held, close Edit through its pinned control, restore the Qt keyboard focus and release those keys. The fixture observes press/release leakage and the next fresh text edit. Internal test queries only locate retained native widget bounds or read state; they cannot activate a control, change focus or mutate the draft.

For delayed dialog closure, the owned QML dialog has a normal button that arms closure after it loses activation. Its eight-second one-shot timer closes through Qt, independent of Park's test hooks. Prepare/reveal the native shortcut entry first, close Edit, open/arm the dialog, then reopen Edit and click the retained shortcut entry. Require capture to own the native seat while the dialog is still mapped. Observe its subsequent unmap while checking native keyboard focus and capture ownership on every presented test step; then cancel through the normal controls and type into the surviving parent. Keep this test of asynchronous application lifetime separate from initial activation or a compositor-injected close. The timer establishes ordering in this debug fixture, not an input-latency budget. V29's shorter two-second interval let the dialog close before capture was established, so it did not prove the required lifetime ordering. The first v23 compilation exposes a fixture borrow mismatch in its generic caption query; that is corrected before the v24 runtime attempt. It provides no new product or runtime acceptance on its own.

V24's **72.93s** failure exposes a fixture navigation mistake: clicking the application in Edit opens Lince's item editor over the intended Shortcuts summary. V25 respects Edit mode, enters/cancels real capture and confirms that Ctrl+F2 key events do not leak, but its **78.31s** run cannot type the first new character after returning to Qt. A separate real Wayland test then isolates a general modifier-notification defect. Focus enters a client while a captured modifier is held; its intercepted release updates Smithay's XKB state but sends no `wl_keyboard.modifiers` event, leaving the client's depressed state stale. The v26 regression fails in **0.01s** for Ctrl before the correction; preserve `ergonomics-toolkit-modifiers-red-v26.log` and the v26 source/executable archive.

The implemented keyboard adapter uses Smithay's existing `input_intercept` and `input_forward` facilities. Forwarded events retain normal key delivery, serials, input authority and LED processing. An intercepted modifier change publishes only the current modifier state to the focused keyboard target when no keyboard grab owns delivery; it never replays the captured key. With an active grab, retain a single `modifiers_pending` bit and pass the current modifiers through the next forwarded event's normal grab path. This preserves input-method/popup-grab ownership instead of delivering modifiers around a grab. All **six keyboard protocol tests pass in 0.08s** at v27 after **22.11s** compilation, including Ctrl/Shift/Alt/Super release after focus return, captured-key isolation, the next ordinary key pair and a real authorized input-method grab receiving deferred state without an update to its underlying application. The Qt workflow now types the fresh `g` with no stuck modifier and no Ctrl/F2 leakage. V27/v28 continue into the next delayed-dialog test but do not yet pass that full scenario; the accepted v31/v32 combined checkpoint is recorded above, and the inspection v7 checkpoint repeats both complete Qt projections on the subsequent inspection source.

The toolkit helper now uses monotonic elapsed milliseconds for input timestamps instead of compressing a long workflow into a few hundred synthetic milliseconds. It waits for the dialog's reported size and actually presented buffer bounds to match its requested configure before clicking. Root and dialog dimensions have separate QML diagnostics. This avoids treating toolkit layout changes or unintended double-click timing as a compositor failure. The native Edit control is reached through its ordinary Show controls hover affordance when collapsed, and application-control clicks assert the presented application target.

The expanded free-mode scenario also demonstrates a separate shared placement bug. At v30 the parent projects to **[542, 374]** while its unmoved dialog projects to **[820, 590]**; the rendered case fails in **44.29s** after **14.26s** compilation. The existing transient solver centers using the parent's canvas-space rectangle, then incorrectly clamps it against screen-space usable bounds. Its failure/source/executable checkpoint is `ergonomics-toolkit-dialog-position-red-v30.log` / `ergonomics-toolkit-v30-*`.

The accepted v31 correction keeps ordinary free-mode dialogs in canvas coordinates: center on the parent plus the retained signed offset, including nested transient previews, without a screen-space clamp. Authored free placement may exist anywhere on the canvas, and changing outputs or camera position must not relocate it. Tiled placement and a free workspace with active maximize/fullscreen still use their existing normalized output-space limits, matching the workspace's existing view-normalization policy. Do not move the parent or rewrite its authored size to make a dialog visible. The focused model tests cover negative canvas positions, changed/offset output bounds, nested signed offsets, preview isolation and retained tiled/maximized/fullscreen limits. Both real Qt projections pass the parent/dialog-center and complete native-capture/close/return checks. Oversized free dialogs retain their authored movement; oversized tiled dialogs retain normalized output-space limits without changing the client's supported logical size.

The toolkit acceptance fixture is `server/tests/manipulation/tiled/toolkit.rs`, with the owner-authored Qt Quick application in `crates/park/fixtures/toolkit.qml`. It runs an externally supplied Qt 6.8+ `qml` executable selected by `LINCE_PARK_TEST_QML`; the local cached Qt 6.11.2 tools are an initial test provider, not a Lince runtime dependency or a path embedded in product code. Keep QML import/plugin setup with that external tool. The application connects only to the fixture's private Park socket, uses private configuration/cache directories and an unavailable private D-Bus address, and its process guard stops/reaps the child on success or panic. Preserve its log and final composed captures under the selected `LINCE_PARK_TEST_ARTIFACTS` directory.

Drive ordinary Qt buttons and fields through Park's actual pointer/keyboard seat. A button creates a separate `Popup.Window` menu; another opens a `Qt.Dialog` transient with window modality and requests activation through Qt. Type into that dialog, close it through its own button and verify both the parent's compositor keyboard focus and actual text mutation recovery. The fixture's title bar invokes Qt's standard system-move request. These are real toolkit paths documented by [Qt popup types](https://doc.qt.io/qt-6/qml-qtquick-controls-popup.html), [transient windows](https://doc.qt.io/qt-6/qml-qtquick-window.html) and [system move/resize](https://doc.qt.io/qt-6.8/qml-qtquick-window.html#startSystemMove-method), not product behavior selected by application name.

The pump must use `Desktop::accept` followed by normal composition and presentation, so requested sizes/states reach the external client and presented-frame focus/input barriers retire normally. Qt acknowledges its own configures and submits its own buffers. The earlier synthetic-client helper deliberately adopted worker frames and supplied its own client buffers; using that helper unchanged for an autonomous toolkit skipped the production configure path. Keep the shared neighboring-client pixel verifier, and separately verify popup pixels and any actual popup occlusion instead of treating a changed image alone as proof of a correctly painted menu. Send a brief key's press/release before an expensive render; deliberately holding the key across rendering legitimately triggers the toolkit's advertised repeat policy and is a separate test.

Early toolkit failures distinguish fixture corrections from product defects. V1 source checking/compilation pass in **5.41s / 19.05s**, but runtime fails in **46.21s** because default Qt logging bypassed the captured diagnostics. V2's **48.69s** failure holds a key across rendering and observes legitimate repeats, while its pump skips client configures. V3 corrects both, then its **9.86s** failure shows why whole-image inequality is insufficient for popup acceptance. V4's **48.96s** failure also assumed Qt Menu supported Home selection. The fixture now resets its own menu selection, uses Down/Enter, verifies a fresh confirmation for each opening and requires both protocol and presented popup teardown. A wide menu exercises painting outside the parent's rectangle, with expected background pixels in every visible quadrant. Preserve those failed logs/captures without labelling fixture assumptions as production bugs.

V5 exposes a real stale-popup defect: Qt confirms/closes its menu and destroys only the xdg-popup role, retaining the wl-surface, but Park keeps painting the previous window snapshot. `XdgShellHandler::popup_destroyed` now uses `State::changed`, because removal changes the window's popup list as well as requiring repaint. It preserves the parent's buffer and old frozen snapshots; it neither destroys the client's surviving surface nor forces a parent resize. The new real-protocol regression fails at the unchanged revision in **0.01s** on v6 and passes on v7. Its captured red executable/source archive is under `ergonomics-toolkit-v6-*`.

V7 then exposes the first post-popup click hitting the previous control. Smithay's ended `PopupPointerGrab::motion` unsets the grab and restores its old pointer location without forwarding that first new motion. `Server::pointer_motion` now retires an ended popup pointer grab before submitting the new event, only when the pointer's current grab serial matches that popup grab. Preserve this guard so a later management/drag grab is not cancelled by an old popup record:

```rust
if self.state.popups.grab.as_ref().is_some_and(|grab| {
    grab.has_ended() && pointer.has_grab(grab.serial())
}) {
    pointer.unset_grab(&mut self.state, SERIAL_COUNTER.next_serial(), time);
}
```

The real-protocol v8 regression receives **[45, 55] instead of [151, 173]** and fails in **0.01s**; its v9 counterpart passes. V11 passes **eight popup/protocol regressions in 0.07s**, including a replacement management grab after legitimate popup retirement; two GPU cases remain ignored in that ordinary selection. V10's replacement-grab fixture initially tried to start management before retiring the old grab, which the normal API correctly rejects. The corrected test sends the first motion before starting management, then verifies that subsequent motion retains the new grab. Red v8 source/executable archives remain inspectable under `ergonomics-toolkit-v8-*`. Current feature/GUI source gates and broad regression acceptance for these production changes remain open.

The v9/v11 Qt workflow paints and dismisses both button-opened and right-click menus, verifies out-of-parent popup pixels and delivers the next ordinary click. Its natural dialog-focus failures (**51.30s / 51.04s**) expose another general input boundary: the private v11 trace shows Qt supplying the parent's **mouse-release serial** to xdg-activation, while Park records only presses. The v12 release-driven protocol regression fails in **0.01s**, with no activation instead of the target surface; its source/executable archive remains under `ergonomics-toolkit-v12-*`.

Paired-release authorization is implemented in `server/input.rs`. Each delivered press retains its trigger identity and stable input root. A matching delivered release on that root aliases its serial to the original authority's timestamp; it does not refresh the five-second expiry or increment the input epoch. Storage remains bounded to 64 entries. Interception, cancellation, unmatched/repeated release, a new press or a different source cannot create a matching release authority. Preserve a foreground token minted on the press. All **ten activation/security regressions pass in 5.32s** at v13, including pointer/keyboard release tokens, intercepted gestures, expiry, bounds, lock and newer-input revocation. The actual Qt dialog then activates, accepts text and closes back to its parent's compositor focus. The parent text field is clicked before typing again: Park restores the application window, while Qt owns internal widget focus.

The later fixture corrects two coordinate assumptions. Use the presented window's actual logical bounds to map toolkit control coordinates into `ItemGeometry.mapping.surface`, including retained free-size scaling. Switching from tiling restores the authored free-canvas view; use the ordinary Focus shortcut afterward rather than forcing a camera in the test. V16 passes the full existing perspective workflow but its flat variant times out starting a client move. V17's direct presented-target assertion isolates the problem: Qt surface 4 is focused, but surfaces 2, 1 and 3 paint and hit above it at the same restored free rectangle. The red source/executable checkpoint is `ergonomics-toolkit-v17-*`.

The shared selection policy extends `topology/stacking.rs::Order`, not a compositor-only raise list. For floating application items, derive an active-family priority from that workspace's focused application and its bounded transient-parent chain. Apply the priority within the existing coplanar order and keep descendants above their parent. The same key feeds visual ordering, native pointer hits and published application geometry; physical plane/depth remains authoritative. Keep authored positions, sizes and `ZIndex` unchanged, exclude native selection and invalid/cross-workspace parent chains, and retain the original order after explicit Unfocus. All **three focused policy tests pass** at v18 after **3m13s** compilation. Broader source/render/GUI and actual coplanar-client acceptance remain open.

V18 still passes only the perspective Qt case (**32.91s** for both cases). V20's failure-path-only world inspection explains the remaining flat target mismatch: physics is enabled in the flat setup but disabled in the perspective setup, and the coincident bodies have different elevations, approximately **480–871** in that run. This is not a coplanar stack-order comparison, and bringing the lower window above physical occluders would violate the preserved-depth contract. The deterministic toolkit fixture now sets physics off through the existing workspace preference before creating its windows in both projections; it does not rewrite item elevations or weaken hit/pixel checks. `projected::prepare_flat` shares that setup with the existing perspective preparation. Keep physical/simulation behavior and depth occlusion under their own existing fixtures. The earlier v15/v19 compilation failures are fixture type/private-access errors, not runtime evidence.

Both expanded Qt workflows pass at **toolkit v22 in 55.38s**, after **14.60s** compilation. At 1000×700 in flat and perspective views, they verify real pointer/keyboard field edits; button and right-click menus; expected background pixels in all four visible quadrants of the parent and nested popup; painting outside the parent window; fresh keyboard-confirmed menu actions and complete presented/protocol teardown; natural dialog activation/typing/close/parent recovery; ordinary Mod+T and Focus handoff to the restored free canvas; Qt standard titlebar move and edge resize; modifier move/resize with early modifier release and Escape restoration; no retained seat buttons; and subsequent ordinary clicks. The native Operation handoff accepts `z` in the shell while preserving the Qt field, then returns to real client typing. The final append explicitly uses End to place the caret; v21's **132.21s** failed run assumed Qt retained an insertion caret after another field click, which can select text. No compositor code changes Qt's widget selection behavior.

The v22 run also exercises `LINCE_PARK_TEST_QML_ARGUMENTS` through an external `env` executable with the QML runner supplied as one literal argument. QML lint passes. Final composed wide captures of nested menus and move/resize previews were visually reviewed in `toolkit-v22/review.png`. These are debug correctness checks with the compositor's hardware Vulkan/EGL path; the selected Qt client uses SHM/software rendering. They do not establish client DMA-BUF acceleration, release FPS, mixed-DPI, physical scanout or installed acceptance. `LINCE_PARK_TEST_WAYLAND_TRACE` enables private child diagnostics; bounded panic excerpts do not replace the complete saved log.

Current-source regressions pass: **211 ordinary Park tests in 24.13s** with **74 GPU/provider cases ignored** in that selection; the existing native stacking GPU test in **8.87s**; all **ten popup/output cases in 8.68s**, including the two separately executed GPU cases; **132 topology tests in 17.28s** with its one existing render case ignored; and **128 native layout tests in 1.44s**. Do not add overlapping selections into a unique-test total. All five warning-denied source gates pass: all-feature/all-target Park **18.96s**, exact nested/server-tests **7.04s**, server-only tests **1.83s**, full Park GUI **14.85s** and Sandbox GUI **10.52s**. Evidence uses `ergonomics-toolkit-*-v22.log`; the scoped Rust/manifests/WGSL/QML source archive, source manifest and copied Park/interface test executable checksums remain unchanged after verification. These are not installable source or GUI artifacts. The narrower the window-management reference capture checkbox above and stage 4/delivery remain unfinished.

Validation preparation now accepts an optional `toolkit_qml: Program`, using the same executable normalization, literal argument bounds and `{run}`/`{profile}` expansion as other chosen tools. The fixture field is:

```json
{
  "toolkit_qml": {
    "program": "/absolute/path/to/a/configured-qml-runner",
    "arguments": []
  }
}
```

The implemented `toolkit-interaction-rendering` stage reuses `preparation_step`'s bounded execution, warning policy, logs and capture directory. It supplies the executable via `LINCE_PARK_TEST_QML` and its arguments as a JSON array via `LINCE_PARK_TEST_QML_ARGUMENTS`, then appends the owned QML source path as a separate argument. There is no shell interpolation. A source preparation with a tool selects both ignored projections in release mode; a failed supplied tool/test fails that stage, and both named cases must actually report a pass rather than accepting an empty Cargo selection. The preceding generic `window-manipulation-input` stage skips this optional module so an absent external Qt tool does not break unrelated input acceptance. With no tool, `toolkit-interaction.json` records an explicit unrun reason. Preparing a selected installed artifact records that this source fixture does not validate its executable; retain the actual installed-client workflow. Qt remains an external validation dependency. All **20 validation-tool regressions pass in 0.11s** after **1m27s** compilation, including literal argv/schema, unrun installed/missing-tool reporting and rejection of empty/partial/failed toolkit selections. The complete optimized preparation invocation remains a stage 4 gate; the direct v22 Qt run above is debug acceptance.

The reviewed order remains width presets, MRU switching, opening/closing and simple pointer control. Widths, MRU and the opening/closing foundation now have their shared implementation and focused acceptance; continue with simple pointer control. Add focused verification alongside each change and collect their actual-input, optimized and installed acceptance under stage 4. Do not rebuild completed features or wait for every stages 2–3 family before validating this core workflow. The following UI and lifetime decisions define the implementation:

| Interaction | Required behavior |
| --- | --- |
| Quick recent-window switch | Alt+Tab selects the previous eligible target; releasing Alt commits. Repeating Tab while holding Alt advances, and Shift reverses. With no alternative target, leave the current view intact. |
| Switcher presentation | One compact, scrollable chooser with title/identity and workspace/output context, reusing overview identity, naming, selection and accessibility helpers. Text is sufficient for the first version; reuse an available cached thumbnail only if it requires no additional preview engine. Inactive workspaces remain reachable; minimized items, descendants of minimized parents, hidden group members and protected/unmapped targets are excluded. Preview does not deliver ordinary keystrokes to clients. |
| Switch cancellation and disappearance | Escape restores the original workspace, view and explicit focus/follow state when still valid. If a target disappears, remove its identity from the gesture snapshot and use a deterministic surviving target; never reuse an unrelated entity or surface. Update MRU order on committed selection, not each preview. |
| Opening and activation | Use one placement/activation path for client requests, chosen launch commands and opening rules. Preserve parent ownership for dialogs and valid user-activation context; mapping a background window alone cannot authorize a focus or camera jump. |
| Closing | Request normal application close through the existing protocol; keep native item confirmation/save behavior. Closing a dialog restores its surviving parent, while an ordinary close uses the tested neighboring-item fallback. Closing the last application leaves the authored workspace, Sands and Areas intact. |
| Move preview | Show a single destination outline/slot. Retain source membership until release; a tiled drop reorders/transfers through the same typed actions as keyboard movement. Moving outside the preview does not implicitly float or change workspace layout. |
| Resize | Choose resize edges from the initial pointer position and keep them for the gesture. Free/floating resize edits the rectangle; tiled resize edits the selected item's width or selected row boundary. Respect client limits and preserve other item widths. Persist the accepted result after release. |

Opening policy must resolve the settings reference's open-maximized preference and the separate default width without either continually overriding the other. Reuse the existing Record/assertion resolution for explicit opening rules; do not introduce a second rule-priority system. The window-management reference owns the placement/activation entry point and reads existing properties; stage 2 adds the missing Window properties/rules controls and routes their results to it. This avoids making core opening/closing depend on a rules editor that in turn depends on core placement. Apply the resulting policy once when a new top-level application is first managed:

1. Respect the surface role and parent relationship. Popups remain protocol-owned popups; a transient dialog uses its parent's placement and focus rules rather than occupying a new ordinary row slot by default.
2. Apply explicit owner-authored opening properties through the existing Record/rule path described in stage 2. An explicitly specified normal/maximized/floating state overrides the profile's opening-state default; a rule specifying only a width does not implicitly change that state.
3. Fill unspecified normal geometry from the default opening width, initially half, and unspecified state from the user's application-opening preference. Initial maximization preserves that normal width for restoration. Native Sands retain their authored geometry unless explicitly managed by the corresponding shared layout action.
4. Resolve the final geometry against usable output bounds, row gaps and client minimum/maximum constraints. Preserve the requested width fraction separately from its constrained size. Insert through the existing row transaction without resizing neighbors.
5. Resolve activation separately from placement. Neither a matching rule nor the profile may turn an unauthorized background map into a focus/camera jump. Saved existing placements retain their identity and geometry during workspace restoration; opening defaults are not reapplied every frame, focus change or monitor reconnect.

With 900 logical units of usable width and zero gaps, a new application with a half-width default has a 450-unit normal width. If this user's open-maximized preference applies, it initially occupies the usable maximized area and restores to 450 units on unmaximize. An explicit normal-state rule with a two-thirds width instead opens at 600 units. A client minimum can constrain either normal result without changing the retained fraction. Stage 4 must verify these cases with a real client, including a dialog closing back to its parent and a background launch leaving focus/camera unchanged.

Close recovery is implemented at the actual unmap/destruction boundary. `Presentation::hide_window` captures the window's state, invokes `lifecycle::before_unmap` while the child and parent identities still exist, then removes the render tree and entity. The helper changes the model focus only when that child was focused in the active workspace and its parent is eligible; missing, minimized, cross-workspace or cyclic parents are rejected. It reuses `focus::select` and preserves existing free-camera follow intent. The shared `focus::keyboard_focus` respects a live modal owner, so asynchronous closure cannot take input away from its native editor. A background dialog closing leaves focus alone. Requesting application close still only requests the normal protocol action; delayed or declined close leaves the child intact. Ordinary neighbor fallback remains the existing model's responsibility. Focused checks include an unrelated neighbor, background closure, invalid parents, parent fullscreen and a settings-modal editor.

Authorized application activation enters through the existing server queue and `Presentation::activate_application`. Its workspace/output selection now reuses `transfers::prepare_identity` and `apply_workspace_transfer`, preserving a destination already visible on another output. A valid activation dismisses overview or MRU through that chooser's own controller before changing focus, retaining the existing key quarantine. Minimized targets restore through normal focus. Background mapping without that authority remains placement only. `transients::inherit`/`place` still provide parent workspace ownership and bounded parent-relative geometry. The rendered fixture supplies application textures through the ordinary buffer path; separate private Wayland clients verify protocol/token behavior. Stage 4's actual-runtime client fixture remains necessary rather than inferring a complete runtime pass from either endpoint.

`Workspace::insert_inactive` now reuses normal row/column insertion while retaining the previous focus, including `None`. Native reconciliation uses it for application Castles, including pending ones, and does not run the new-native-item reveal step for those applications. Width/opening policy still runs once. Native Sand creation keeps its existing selection semantics. The focused model/native/rendered checks cover an ordinary focused window, a native child editor, explicit Unfocus, the first application in an empty workspace and pending-to-mapped transitions; a fullscreen fixture now explicitly selects its intended application instead of depending on insertion stealing focus.

Foreground launch commands use the implemented general adapter. `Application::command` removes inherited `XDG_ACTIVATION_TOKEN` values and adds only the fresh token explicitly supplied by Park. `Server::spawn_foreground_helper` covers interactive terminal/launcher/editor concepts, and `spawn_foreground` covers the explicitly requested startup applications in nested and physical modes. Hardware/capture/ready/service helpers retain background `spawn_helper` behavior. The common spawn path preserves connection-scoped capabilities and revokes the fresh token on every failed spawn/setup return.

`launch_activation_token` uses Smithay 0.7's `XdgActivationState::create_external_token`, initializes the existing input-epoch metadata because that API bypasses `token_created`, and shares the five-second lifetime, 128-token bound and one-shot validation with client-issued tokens. Issuance prunes expired/stale epochs; newer input invalidates an outstanding request. Lock entry clears issued tokens and queued activations and advances the epoch, so quick unlock cannot revive an old token. `Server::activations` still waits for actual mapping. No private token map, process-name focus rule or launcher service is added.

The native host uses the foreground helper for an authorized interactive command:

```rust
self.server.spawn_foreground_helper(&description, &socket)
```

Private Wayland-client tests cover trigger press/release before mapping, single use, unknown/expired/stale tokens, newer intercepted or forwarded input, queued-request invalidation, lock/unlock, token capacity and failed helper/socket startup. A real child receives the literal token in its environment and a private client consumes it through the standard activation protocol; an ordinary background helper receives no inherited token. The v5 combined run passes **242 shared tests in 6.69s** and **14 Park launch/protocol/rendered tests in 95.20s** (`ergonomics-opening-functional-v5.log`). The latter includes both opening/closing and MRU rendering at 1000 and 360 pixels. All **189 ordinary Park tests pass in 3.38s**, with **31 GPU cases ignored** in that ordinary run (`ergonomics-opening-park-ordinary-v5.log`); two of those GPU cases pass in the focused run, while the other 29 retain their optimized integration acceptance.

The initial rendered-fixture compilation failure is retained in `ergonomics-opening-functional-v1.log`; the fixture now uses the public window actions rather than a private configuration module. The v2 run exposed the old fullscreen fixture's implicit insertion-focus assumption. The first modal reproduction compiler was terminated with exit 143 before running tests (`ergonomics-opening-modal-red-v3.log`); the serial retry reproduces the modal-focus failure in v4, and the corrected case passes in v5. The source manifest, scoped Rust/manifests/WGSL archive and copied test-executable checksums use the `ergonomics-opening-v5-*` prefix. As with the preceding snapshots, this is test provenance, not an installed GUI artifact or an optimized/physical-session acceptance claim.

The current Park and ordinary Sandbox GUI checks pass with warnings denied in **26.70s / 19.68s** (`ergonomics-opening-{park,sandbox}-gui-v5.log`), and the interface without default features passes in **1.05s** (`ergonomics-opening-portable-v5.log`). All **18 validation-tool tests pass in 0.13s** (`ergonomics-opening-xtask-v5.log`). The release preparation runner now registers `window-opening-rendering` beside `recent-window-rendering`; registering it does not claim a new optimized run. The source manifest remains unchanged after these checks. The current 360-pixel capture is byte-identical to the visually inspected v2 capture; its purpose is scene/buffer continuity, while the assertions establish focus and output ownership. Actual application-menu dialog/input flow remains the separate stage 4 requirement below.

MRU switching must use the registry's configured forward/reverse chords. Alt is the default initiating modifier, not a constant inside the controller: rebinding the gesture changes which held modifier keeps it open and which release commits. Validate that a switching chord includes a usable hold modifier; do not accept plain Tab as a desktop switcher gesture. Capture, settings modals, client shortcut inhibition and session lock retain their existing ownership rules. Keep the first version to the chooser, forward/reverse, commit and cancel; application filtering, search and extra switcher modes are outside suggestion 2.

An effective configured forward/reverse chord takes precedence over the chooser's ordinary arrow/Enter/Space controls. For example, Ctrl+Up assigned to forward switching must advance forward on every distinct press, rather than becoming reverse navigation after the chooser opens. Escape remains the cancellation control and is rejected as an MRU trigger by shared settings validation; mapped logical Escape retains normal emergency-cancel semantics. The chooser always shows the effective configured chords and modifier-release/click behavior, without promising that a deliberately rebound confirmation key still confirms. Preserve Enter/Space confirmation and arrows when they are not the matching configured gesture.

Validate the forward/reverse pair together: they need a common hold modifier, and Shift used only to reverse direction must not commit the chooser when released. Trigger-key auto-repeat does not race through targets. A quick press/release between rendered frames still selects and commits once. Cancellation caused by lock, modal takeover or lost input ownership closes the chooser safely without replaying intercepted keys into an application. Test these through the same event ownership used by normal shortcuts; do not add a second MRU keyboard table.

Retain per-event ownership for the hold modifier as well as Tab. Winit may notify a modifier release before its following `ModifiersChanged` mask update; a keyboard event's captured mask therefore does not always describe the state after that release. Use the existing logical/Park modifier roles and contributor tracking, extending the shared event context only where the controller needs missing transition information. Do not infer every commit from one final-frame mask or hardcode the physical Alt key names. Two complete switching gestures delivered in one update must remain two gestures, while releasing reverse-only Shift or one of two held contributors cannot commit the active gesture prematurely. Preserve this distinction when rebinding the common hold modifier.

The current implementation boundary is `native/layout/desktop/recent.rs`, with a shared target catalogue and native chooser UI in its sibling directory. The public commands are `focus.recent` and `focus.recent-previous`, exposed through `/focus/recent` and `/focus/recent-previous`. Their default chords are Alt+Tab and Alt+Shift+Tab. They use the existing shortcut editor, effective-label helpers and atomic interface configuration; validation checks their shared non-Shift hold modifier as one settings transaction. Operation or another typed invocation without a held gesture performs one immediate switch.

Keep preview selection inside the compact chooser. Opening, advancing and cancelling it do not activate or configure each highlighted application and do not suspend the existing camera follow. Commit resolves the selected stable identity through the normal focus/workspace/output controllers. Target identity contains both the live Bevy entity generation and its placement ID; matching a title or reusing a placement ID after destruction cannot retarget a pending selection. Only eligible top-level items enter the list; parent-owned transients contribute their parent identity, and shared eligibility handles minimized ancestors and unavailable content. Titles/context use the existing overview naming helpers, with bounded sanitized text.

Recent history is ephemeral per canvas root, bounded to 4096 targets, and updated from actual focus changes. A gesture snapshots its order and may prune vanished targets without inserting newly mapped ones. The retained modal uses the existing Sand buttons, Tab group, accessibility labels/selection, scroll/focus-reveal and token styles. It keeps the previous native editor by identity; Cancel restores that surviving editor only in the matching active workspace. An unrelated newer modal owns its own focus and cannot be closed by an old chooser action.

The compositor handoff uses the same chronological keyboard pipeline. When its shortcut registry recognizes an MRU trigger, Park restores the actual held modifier contributors and forwards the raw trigger press/release to the worker, with the normal tracked-input frame barrier. This permits a release to reach the native controller even though the compositor intercepted its press. Client shortcut inhibition, lock and modal ownership still apply. Closing during native dispatch quarantines the event's held keys, and the remaining events in that same batch retire them in order; using the final held set or waiting for an already-processed release would strand input or swallow a later fresh gesture.

Because the canvas worker and compositor acknowledge asynchronously, an MRU commit uses an internal request token rather than a mutable current selection:

```rust
DesktopAction::CommitRecent { request }
```

The shared controller captures the root and exact target against that token in a bounded pending ledger. A subsequent gesture projects those pending selections over observed history, so two quick Alt+Tab gestures still switch out and back before the first host reply. `recent::selection(world, root, request)` validates the latest target membership; Park reuses `transfers::prepare_identity` and its staged output assignment, then calls `recent::activate` or rejects through `recent::acknowledge`. A failed queue or missing destination does not promote the target. Applying an older commit while a newer chooser is open retains native keyboard ownership, updates that chooser's workspace/output return context and restores its highlighted row's input focus. Internal commit tokens are not an additional configurable command or an automation API.

Keep the shared workspace input cache coherent through that overlap, not just MRU's per-target editor cache. Closing the newer chooser restores the previous committed native editor before a later host commit; v6 verifies ordinary return after both commits. The additional cancellation case exposes the source workspace's cache: `focus::remember_input` must preserve it when actual input focus belongs to the active keyboard modal. Replacing the remembered editor with `None` while leaving that workspace loses editor focus on a later normal return after the newer gesture is cancelled. The v8 implementation checks the existing modal owner and input ancestry before updating that shared cache. Existing target/workspace/field validation still decides whether a remembered editor is valid; there is no second workspace-input cache.

The v5 combined run passed **234 shared model/native/shortcut/configuration/Operation/keyboard tests in 6.59s** and the **wide/narrow MRU hardware GPU case in 73.68s**, after a **14m56s** cold compilation. `ergonomics-recent-functional-v5.log` and the v5 source manifest identify that checkpoint. Its captures at 1000 and 360 pixels, including the scrolled long-title list at both widths, were visually inspected and are readable. The v6 rendered extension additionally passes ordinary workspace return after two committed gestures in **73.04s** (`ergonomics-recent-return-debug-v6.log`). These use mapped application textures, retained native text and actual keyboard/pointer dispatch inside the presentation worker.

The v7 native-editor cancellation regression reproduced ordinary workspace return selecting the wrapper instead of its surviving editor (`ergonomics-recent-cancel-return-v7.log`). The shared modal-ancestry guard fixes that case, and the broader v8 run passes **235 shared tests in 6.48s** plus the **wide/narrow GPU case in 73.78s**, after **1m20s** compilation (`ergonomics-recent-functional-v8.log`). The ordinary Park suite passes **183 tests in 5.43s**, explicitly leaving **30 GPU cases ignored** (`ergonomics-recent-park-ordinary-v8.log`); the new MRU GPU case ran separately, while the other 29 retain final current-source acceptance in stage 4.

Both GUI source checks pass with warnings denied: **3m37s** for shipping `instinct,park` and **3m17s** for ordinary Sandbox with `instinct`. Portable interface checking passes in **6.00s**. Logs are `ergonomics-recent-{park-gui,sandbox-gui,portable}-v8.log`. The frozen copied executables, scoped Rust/manifests/WGSL archive and verified source/executable/archive checksums use `ergonomics-recent-v8-*`. The archive omits assets/toolchains and is not an installable source package; these debug test executables are not an installed GUI candidate. No owner configuration, active desktop or system activation changed.

All **18 validation-tool regressions pass in 0.13s** (`ergonomics-recent-xtask-v8.log`), including the existing runner's helper authorization, artifact identity, cleanup, timeout and physical-terminal contracts. The current scoped source and frozen executable/archive checksums still match after these checks.

The v8 wide/narrow initial captures are byte-identical to the inspected v5 captures. The current narrow scrolled capture was also inspected and keeps the selected row, wrapped titles and context readable inside the clip. Reproduce the combined software/rendered run using one feature graph when later changes require it:

```bash
nix develop .#interface --command bash -c '
  export RUSTFLAGS="-C link-arg=-fuse-ld=mold -C target-cpu=native"
  export LINCE_PARK_TEST_ARTIFACTS="$PWD/target/park-validation/recent-window-review"
  cargo test --locked -p lince-interface -p lince-park \
    --features lince-park/session,lince-park/server-tests --lib -- \
    layout::desktop:: native::shortcuts:: native::operation:: native::keyboard:: \
    presentation::tests::recent:: --include-ignored --test-threads=1
'
```

The passing rendered fixture covers mapped application textures through the ordinary buffer path, not a separate live Wayland client connection. Actual compositor/IME/client routing remains stage 4 acceptance. The existing preparation runner now contains `recent-window-rendering`, selecting this case in release mode for the final optimized pass.

For pointer interactions, reuse the existing Smithay pointer-grab and native item-action paths with one interaction owner. Route motion/release/cancel to that owner until completion; lock, target unmap or output removal must cancel safely. An intercepted modifier-drag must not also start a client click, text selection or context menu, and cancellation must restore the original geometry/membership without stuck buttons. Keep the preview transient; do not write intermediate arrangements to persistence on every motion.

Acquire the gesture from the modifier/button state at the initial press over an eligible managed top-level item. Popups, external bars and other shell surfaces retain their existing interaction ownership. Releasing the modifier before the mouse button does not turn the intercepted press into a client click: retain the grab until button release or cancellation. After cancellation, quarantine any still-held intercepted button until release. A later ordinary right-click must arrive as one complete press/release pair, including its normal popup authorization. Cover this sequence without application-name exceptions.

Keep suggestion 5 small: one target, one move or resize gesture and one destination preview. Reuse existing hit testing, projection and constraints; no new gesture framework, multi-selection, inertial throwing, snapping zones or automatic float-on-detach behavior. The first version moves an item within its current workspace; cross-workspace/monitor movement remains available through the typed commands, with overview dragging added separately in stage 2. Mouse modifier/button choices belong to the shared configuration/editor as typed pointer triggers, with the same conflict feedback and atomic save; do not encode a mouse button as a keyboard key or create private Park-only bindings. Existing unmodified Sand editing and application context menus remain usable.

The pointer bindings and shared gesture controller are implemented; the remaining real-client/toolkit acceptance is listed below. The sources provide `pointer.move` / `pointer.resize`, defaulting to Mod+MouseLeft / Mod+MouseRight, and a typed `Trigger::Pointer(PointerChord)` beside the existing keyboard `Chord`. `Settings::chord` returns keyboard triggers only; `Settings::pointer` and `shortcuts::pointer_bindings` expose the mouse variants. The existing configuration stores the canonical binding text under the registered command ID and validates it according to that command's trigger type. Plain or Shift-only mouse bindings are rejected to preserve ordinary clicks, and two equivalent mouse triggers conflict in the same batch editor. Configuration/capture and runtime gesture ownership remain separate responsibilities; the native and Park adapters below consume these same configured triggers.

Input preparation retains mouse-button context in `native/keyboard/pointer.rs`. It matches the chronological `WindowEvent` stream with the existing prepared keyboard events and uses their authoritative `after` modifier masks, including host keymap roles; native Winit button snapshots cover input with no preceding key event. A press before a modifier release and a button release afterward keep distinct contexts even in one update. The binding editor captures modified mouse buttons through that context, preserves its draft until Validate, and quarantines the captured button through release. Filtering must retain only fresh unowned pointer events and advance its cursor after publishing them; re-enqueuing previous-frame clicks would replay actions, and synthesizing a general pointer cancellation would affect unrelated button state. Keep capture before `PickingSystems::ProcessInput` and extend the existing ownership path rather than adding another keyboard dispatcher.

The portable layout core in `layout/desktop/gestures.rs` provides `Gesture::begin` and `Gesture::propose`. A proposal is a model copy plus the prospective rectangle. It reuses the current row members, widths, layout solver and constraints; moving a tiled item inserts the existing member beside an anchor or into a new row. Free moves preserve normal geometry and size intent, and free resize retains the opposite edge while clamping client limits. A target change invalidates the proposal, while an unrelated new item is retained by starting each proposal from the latest current model. The core itself does not publish or persist the result; the shared native controller now owns that transaction:

```rust
let gesture = Gesture::begin(&workspace.desktop, id, Manipulation::Move, uv, usable)?;
let (proposed, preview) = gesture.propose(&workspace.desktop, delta, slot)?;
```

The v6 foundation checkpoint passes **263 shared model/native/shortcut/configuration/keyboard/notification tests in 6.70s** and **both hardware GPU shortcut scenarios in 160.42s** (`ergonomics-pointer-functional-v6.log`). The mouse scenario drives capture, modifier release before button release, conflict review, inline reassignment, Save, reopening and one ordinary right-click at 1000 and 360 pixels. The 360-pixel review capture was visually inspected. The existing keyboard capture scenario still passes its activation-frame input, same-batch validation, wide/narrow controls and save checks. These fixtures verify configuration and capture, not movement of a live application window.

The earlier rendered failure is retained: adding rows required the keyboard fixture to scroll to its intended control rather than assume its position. The new save/reopen case then exposed a passive toast covering the edit panel at 360 pixels (`pointer-v4-captures/pointer-binding-missing-modal-360.png`). Passive toasts now sit below the modal edit panel; their history, retained entities and keyboard focus are preserved. The v6 test reopens through the same pointer control without dismissing the toast. The v5 test-only import failure is retained in its log and corrected in v6. Mouse capture filters fresh owned events rather than sending an unrelated pointer cancellation or replaying old clicks.

All **189 ordinary Park tests pass in 4.63s**, with **32 GPU tests ignored** in that ordinary run (`ergonomics-pointer-park-ordinary-v6.log`); the two configuration/capture GPU cases pass separately above. Park/Sandbox GUI checks pass with warnings denied in **19.32s / 14.87s**, and the interface without default features passes in **2.00s** (`ergonomics-pointer-{park,sandbox}-gui-v6.log`, `ergonomics-pointer-portable-v6.log`). All **18 validation-tool tests pass in 0.11s**. The preparation runner registers `pointer-binding-rendering` for optimized acceptance. The `ergonomics-pointer-v6-*` source manifest, scoped Rust/manifests/WGSL archive and test-executable checksums identify this checkpoint and remain unchanged after those checks. They are test provenance rather than an installed or optimized GUI artifact. The complete drag feature, actual-runtime client input and installed/physical acceptance remain open.

The remaining model contract is implemented. Pointer sizing shares the keyboard sizing solver through `resize_item`, while the public keyboard step retains its 10,000-unit bound; a pointer proposal can reach the supported layout/client limit. Zero motion, an inactive resize axis and a tiled move without a destination leave the complete model and maximize state unchanged. A shared bottom edge changes its row height; the next row's top edge changes that same preceding boundary. The outer top edge stays anchored. Widths, other row heights and float/retile homes remain independent. Current client limits resolve before edge capture, world-coordinate limits clamp safely, and changes to the target, source row/width, active shared boundary, gap or influence reject a stale proposal. Unrelated free-layout tiling preferences and unrelated window changes survive. The 19 model gesture tests pass as part of the native v3 checkpoint below; do not reimplement this contract in the input adapters.

The shared native transaction now lives in `native/layout/desktop/manipulation.rs`. `begin` captures the exact entity generation, placement, root/workspace, output viewport and transient parent, then suspends camera follow. `propose` builds against current committed state and installs separate `canvas::ItemPreview` components. Free/floating movement and resize have live visual geometry; a tiled move keeps original membership and one retained, noninteractive insertion outline. Stationary updates compare their model basis instead of recreating that outline or rewriting identical preview components. The existing physics ownership check holds the target and affected preview items. `commit` regenerates, changes the model/rules and affected authored geometry once, then uses shared focus selection. `cancel` removes presentation state and restores surviving view/input/follow through the existing helper, preserving a newer modal's input. An unmap/replacement cannot redirect the captured entity to another window.

The v11 correction introduces shared `native::canvas_item::Ephemeral` for presentation-only canvas decorations. The tiled insertion outline carries it, and external-drop `Preview` requires it. Authored canvas/workspace/content snapshots exclude the marker before assigning permanent placement identities; layout eligibility and both physics paths exclude it too. Rendering still displays the decoration. An original dragged item receives `ItemPreview`, not `Ephemeral`: capture must retain that original's committed placement while ignoring the visual proposal. Promotion of a dropped preview into real content explicitly removes both `Preview` and `Ephemeral`. Preserve this distinction in future previews rather than using an unrelated feature's preview marker or omitting every item under manipulation. The regression captures a real editable Sand with an active visual proposal alongside ephemeral decorations/copies and requires an unchanged authored snapshot with no newly assigned decoration identities. The v11 session/test source check passes with warnings denied in **2m27s**; the subsequent v12 test run includes a normal Focus command in the visible-Refresh fixture and retains its own source checkpoint.

At cell-preview v12, test compilation passes with warnings denied in **7m22s** and **all 103 selected shared regressions pass in 56.75s** (`ergonomics-cell-preview-compile-v12.log`, `ergonomics-cell-preview-interface-v12.log`). They cover canvas capture/restore including the new ephemeral regression, real drop promotion/cancellation and saved content, simple pointer transactions, Area effects and both physics paths. Copied `cell-preview-v12-{interface,park}-tests` executables/checksums and the scoped `ergonomics-cell-preview-v12-*` source manifest/archive identify this checkpoint; the source checksum check remains clean after compilation. These debug tests are not an installed GUI artifact. Both new hardware GPU cases pass in **43.91s** (`ergonomics-cell-preview-gpu-v12.log`). Free resize and tiled move complete timed autosave, a real stopped/replaced local channel, full old Engine release and reopening the same file-backed Cell/Presentation. The wide/narrow recovery case uses actual pointer press/release on the visible native Refresh control, requires exactly one activation and retains the unrelated native draft. The current tiled preview/reopen and 1000/360-pixel recovery captures in `cell-preview-v12/` were visually inspected. Retain actual-client, optimized, selected-provider and physical acceptance below.

All **201 ordinary Park tests pass in 16.14s** at v12; that run leaves **52 GPU cases ignored**, with only the two new persistence/recovery cases run separately above. All-feature/all-target Park checking passes with warnings denied in **1m05s**; full Park/Sandbox GUI checks pass in **4m28s / 4m56s**. Portable interface, minimal Park and server-only Park checks pass in **13.45s / 27.53s / 17.51s**. All **18 validation-tool regressions pass in 0.11s** after **2m30s** compilation. Logs use `ergonomics-cell-preview-{park-ordinary,all-targets,park-gui,sandbox-gui,portable,base,server,xtask}-v12.log`. The scoped source checksum check remains clean after every command completes. These are debug functional and source-boundary results, not optimized pacing, an installed GUI package or physical/session acceptance. No active desktop, owner configuration or system activation changed.

Rendering consumes `canvas::presented`/`ItemPreview` through ordinary canvas projection, viewport clipping, native surface capture, topology output geometry and desktop publication. Park's normal window-configuration path uses that presented size and the controller's temporary `window_state`; a client can therefore receive preview configure sizes without writing them into the saved workspace. No authored `CanvasItem` or layout rule is temporarily changed just to measure or display the preview. Dialog placement uses a committed parent-relative offset in `transients::Placement`, with an optional proposal override during rendering. Cancelling does not install that offset; after commit, moving a surviving parent retains it. The later native input source also holds transient ancestors during the gesture, rejects explicit parent changes and retains oversized dialogs' supported logical sizes through positioning/clipping. Complete real-client acknowledgement and projected-input acceptance remain below.

These are the implemented controller entry points. Native pointer input, Park's modifier-drag binding and authorized standard application requests use the common projection adapter:

```rust
use crate::native::layout::desktop::manipulation;

let started = manipulation::begin(world, root, target, Manipulation::Move, uv);
if started {
    manipulation::propose(world, total_layout_delta, slot);
}
```

An accepted release must first update with its matching pointer position/stamp, then call `manipulation::commit(world)`. Cancellation calls `manipulation::cancel(world)`. The input adapter must retain gesture ownership and quarantine independently of whether a proposal is still active; removing a preview does not authorize delivering a previously intercepted press to a client.

The native v3 checkpoint passes **307 shared model/controller/shortcut/keyboard/notification/canvas/viewport/topology tests in 9.19s**, followed by the new rendered application scenario in **18.55s** (`ergonomics-manipulation-native-v3.log`; compilation **4m25s**). The rendered scenario covers move, cancellation, resize and commit at 1000/360 pixels in flat and spatial views, checks application pixels outside the original footprint, verifies unchanged authored model/geometry during preview, and checks the temporary requested client size. It uses submitted application textures and direct controller calls; it does not exercise a live Wayland client's input, configure acknowledgement or modifier-drag through the desktop worker. The v2 failures were three fresh-free-canvas fixture initialization errors; the fixture now enters the same existing management setup before capturing its baseline. Do not weaken runtime eligibility to make an uninitialized fixture pass. The preparation runner registers `window-manipulation-rendering` for later release acceptance; registration is not a release pass.

All **189 ordinary Park tests pass in 3.29s**, with **33 GPU cases ignored** in that ordinary run (`ergonomics-manipulation-native-park-ordinary-v3.log`); the new controller rendering case passes separately above. Park and Sandbox GUI source checks pass with warnings denied in **54.42s / 54.13s**, the interface without default features passes in **2.11s**, and the **18 validation-tool tests pass in 0.14s** (`ergonomics-manipulation-native-{park,sandbox}-gui-v3.log`, `ergonomics-manipulation-native-portable-v3.log`, `ergonomics-manipulation-native-xtask-v3.log`). Narrow flat-move and spatial-resize captures were inspected: the flat fixture includes a window straddling the viewport's upper edge; the spatial fixture shows the resized surface. These are isolated debug-controller checks, not a complete desktop interaction or performance claim. The `ergonomics-manipulation-native-v3-*` source manifest, scoped Rust/manifests/WGSL archive and copied test-executable checksums verify this source checkpoint before and after the checks. They are not an installed GUI candidate. Ordinary and optimized actual-input, real-client/provider and physical acceptance stay open.

The native input source is now present in `native/layout/desktop/manipulation/pointer.rs` and `pointer/native.rs`, with one shared `native/pointer_hit.rs` helper also used by shortcut capture. It resolves the displayed face and exact target identity, freezes the resize projection, uses the existing spatial plane helper for movement, and intercepts configured button gestures before ordinary picking. Native event handling retains each button's matching cursor position, modifier-release behavior, Escape/focus-loss cancellation and held-button quarantine. Separate `SpatialPreview` presentation preserves the opposite corner of rotated/scaled resizes; maximize restoration keeps the grabbed point and avoids a release-time camera jump. Preview-owned Fit sizing does not write measured transient dimensions into the authored item. These source changes do not establish external application dragging through Smithay.

Explicit application resize edges are supported by the same shared model/controller. `Gesture::begin_with_edges` and `manipulation::begin_with_edges` accept an optional `[i8; 2]` mask: -1 selects the leading edge, 1 the trailing edge and 0 leaves that axis unchanged. Reject an empty/invalid mask or one attached to a move operation. The existing native modifier gesture retains its initial-hit edge selection. Single-axis free/floating resize preserves the other axis and its sizing rule; tiled top/bottom masks resolve the existing shared row boundary independently of hit position. The future protocol adapter must perform its seat/client/serial validation before this alternative entry point, for example for the right edge:

```rust
let started = manipulation::begin_with_edges(
    world, root, target, Manipulation::Resize, uv, Some([1, 0]),
);
```

The native-input v4 checkpoint passes **323 shared regressions in 10.59s** and **all four hardware GPU cases in 215.47s**, after **3m24s** compilation (`ergonomics-pointer-input-functional-v4.log`). The native modifier-event case covers rotated Sands at 1000/360 pixels in flat/spatial views: same-update input, live movement, early modifier release, fixed-opposite-corner resize, Escape cancellation, held-button quarantine and a subsequent ordinary context click. The separate world Mouse and content-pointer domains each receive their intended ordinary event once, with neither receiving owned drag presses. The direct application-texture scenario, pointer binding conflict/save/reopen and keyboard capture scenarios also pass. Wide flat-resize, narrow flat-move/spatial-resize and narrow shortcut-review captures were visually inspected. The save/reload regression now passes through the ordinary snapshot loader and compares the complete saved and reloaded document with the committed scene during an active free resize or tiled move. The v3 failure was the test reading the older single-file path with `read_snapshot`, while current persistence writes the snapshot directory loaded by `read_document`; the product save transaction is unchanged. That failed run retains **318 passes / one failure** in its log. The earlier v2 ordinary-click assertion confused the distinct pointer domains; v4 verifies each target explicitly. Retain those failure logs rather than treating them as current failures or suppressing the delivery assertions.

The native-input v4 test-source check passes with warnings denied in **33.04s**.
